Deliberately not taken in the effort that found it: it changes a destructive verb's behavior and its reporting contract, which wants its own red-first test per site rather than a tail-of-section edit, and section 3 was a documentation pass. What section 3 did instead was write the behavior down, in `memory-system/SKILL.md` and in `docs/security-model.md`, so an operator meets it in the documentation rather than in a lost backup.

- **The stopped-delete pin in `test/memq.test.js` carries a dead assertion whose message contradicts the comment above it (2026-08-22, from the shared-tier authoring effort's section 3).** The `.bak` non-existence check near the end of "a delete of a name the tier does not hold spends no backup on the sidecar" cannot fail: the closing sweep unlinks the sidecar backup on every completed run, whoever wrote it, which is exactly what the comment three lines above now says. The real discriminator in that test is the byte-identity assertion that follows it. The assertion is therefore not merely redundant, it asserts the opposite of the comment, so whoever narrows the sweep later reads its failure message as evidence for a rule the file elsewhere denies. Fix is to drop it or re-point it at a fact the sweep does not erase. Do not confuse it with the neighbouring "a completed delete leaves no backup holding the record it removed", whose two `.bak` assertions are the live red for the closing sweep and must stay. Not taken here because section 3 held its code and test edits to comments, and changing an assertion is a test-logic change that belongs with the sweep work in the entry above it.

- **The shared-tier delete sweeps the `.bak` at its three index and usage targets but not a `.tmp.<pid>` stranded at the same paths, and the two documents describing that residue now carry the exception rather than an absolute (2026-08-22, from the shared-tier authoring effort's section 3).** `plugins/claude-kit/scripts/memq.js` closes a completed `delete-type` or `delete-operator` by unlinking `<path>.bak` at the tier index, the archive index and the usage sidecar. A rewrite killed between its `writeFileSync(tmp, ...)` and its `renameSync` strands `MEMORY.md.tmp.<pid>` at one of those paths, holding the index as it then stood: for a record authored without a body flag the index line carries that record's whole stored body, which is the same reason the `.bak` is swept. The record-side sweep already handles both shapes (`recordCopyEntry` matches `.bak` and `/^\.tmp\.\d+$/`), so the asymmetry is between the two sweeps in one verb rather than a missing concept. The shape of the fix is to extend the closing sweep to the temp shape at the same three paths, which needs the tail test factored out of `recordCopyEntry`, today bound to a record-name comparison the index sweep cannot make, so that both sweeps share one rule rather than minting a second, and then to restore the absolute wording the prose gave up. Not urgent, and deliberately not taken in the effort that found it: reaching the state needs a hard kill (SIGKILL or power loss) between two adjacent calls, the residue never leaves the machine because `*.tmp.*` is one of the sync's never-sync patterns, and closing it is a behavior change to a destructive verb, which wants its own red-first test per site rather than a tail-of-section edit. What section 3 did instead was bound the claim: `docs/architecture.md` and `docs/security-model.md` now say no copy the verb can name survives and name this one as the exception, so the documents are true of today's code and this entry is what makes them absolute again.

- **The hook canary's grant probes are bounded per child, so the phase they form has no bound at all (2026-08-22, from the shared-tier authoring effort's section 2).** `plugins/claude-kit/hooks/hook-canary.js` spawns six grant probes on Windows and five elsewhere, each capped at `PROBE_TIMEOUT_MS = 5000`, so the worst case before integrity hashing is about 35 seconds. That worst case is reached exactly when something is wrong, which is the moment a session start hanging half a minute reads as the kit being broken rather than the kit reporting, and it sits on the path a user waits on at every session start. The shape of the fix is a whole-phase wall-clock bound rather than a per-child one, with the probes that did not get to run reported as unrun rather than passed over in silence, which is what keeps the honesty property the probes exist for: a probe phase that quietly stops early would report a healthy grant hook on evidence it never gathered. It was deliberately not taken in the effort that grew the count, because it changes the canary's reporting contract rather than its correctness, and a reporting-contract change wants its own enumeration of what every consumer of a probe result does with an unrun one. Not urgent: nothing here is wrong, the cost is latency in the failure case, and each added probe was added to close a real detector gap.

- **A resumed archive name can carry its description onto a different retired record's archive index line (2026-08-22, from the shared-tier authoring effort's review round 10).** `carryArchiveIndex` in `plugins/claude-kit/scripts/memq.js` always writes the archive index line for a name the pass is retiring, including a name admitted as the resume of an earlier stopped run. The resume admission now requires three facts (a genuine ENOENT at the record path, a plain file in the archive slot, and the tier index still listing the name), which closes the case the review found. What remains needs all three to hold about two different memories at once: a live record gone from the tier while its index line survives, and an unrelated retired record already sitting under that name in `archive/`. The carry then overwrites the retired record's description with the live one's. No body is ever confused and no record is lost; one archive index line describes the wrong memory. Two paths reach the precondition, and the second is the one that keeps this from being pure operator error: a hand deletion of a record file, which the shared tiers bar but cannot prevent, and a partial or conflicted sync merge that carries a `delete-type`/`delete-operator` removal of the record without the matching index-line removal. The fix is to have the carry skip a name whose archive index line already exists when that name arrived as a resume. It was deliberately not taken in the effort that found it, because it changes the carry's contract rather than the validator's and wants its own enumeration of the archive pass's stop states: a genuine stop after the carry landed and before the rename would then be a name whose line exists and whose description the pass declines to refresh, which is believed harmless and is not proven so. Not urgent, and bounded to a description.

- **The memory-store destination probe can silently mislead in two directions, and neither is recorded as unproven (2026-08-21, from the sync-state-writer-and-push-pair effort's review round).** `Get-MemorySyncStatus` in `plugins/claude-kit/doctor/install-memory-sync.ps1` reads git through `Invoke-MemorySyncGit`, which merges stderr into stdout, and then takes `Output[0]`. Five reads in that function do this, including the branch, the upstream, and the two destination reads added for the push-pair check. A git warning printed ahead of the value is therefore read AS the value, which for a config read means the check either never fires or fires with a warning string standing in for a branch name. The kit already knows this hazard and solved it the other way in `plugins/claude-kit/doctor/sync-store.ps1`, which takes `Output[-1]` and says why in a comment on each site, so the two files disagree about the same helper. Separately, when either destination read simply fails, nothing records that it failed: an unreadable `push.default` silently falls back to git's default, and an unreadable upstream ref silently disables the name-pair check altogether while the report still prints a confident destination line. Every other probe in that file has an unverified state (`DestinationRead`, `RemoteBranchesRead`, `ProbesRan`); these do not. Both halves want one pass over the same function: switch the reads to the last-line convention with a shape filter, and give the pair a read flag that feeds the existing unverified-destination WARN. Left out of the effort that surfaced it because fixing only the two newest reads would leave three older ones carrying the same defect, which is half a class fixed and worse than none. Not urgent: `git config --get` does not warn on a healthy repository, and no instance has been observed.

- **The push-destination check assumes a central workflow and would misreport a triangular one (2026-08-21, from the sync-state-writer-and-push-pair effort's review round).** The check added to `Get-MemorySyncDestinationLines` in `plugins/claude-kit/doctor/doctor.ps1` reads `push.default` and compares the local branch name against its upstream's. Git only enforces that pairing when the push remote and the fetch remote are the same. With `remote.pushDefault` or `branch.<name>.pushRemote` naming a different remote, git behaves as though `push.default` were `current` and pushes a mismatched pair happily, so the doctor would emit a blocking FAIL and exit nonzero over a store whose pushes work. That is the failure mode the same function's own header comment warns about, since a check that fails over a state the operator chose teaches them to stop reading the section. It also means the real triangular hazard, pushes landing on a remote that the pulls never read, goes unnamed. The fix is to read the two push-remote keys and either skip the name-pair finding or reword it when the push remote differs from the fetch remote. Reachability is close to nil for a personal memory store, which is why it was left out rather than folded in.

- **`Write-SyncState` cannot report its own failure, so the defect class the null-marshalling fix closed would recur invisibly (2026-08-21, from the sync-state-writer-and-push-pair effort).** The state write in `plugins/claude-kit/doctor/sync-store.ps1` sits inside a `try` whose `catch` deletes the temp file and returns: no output, no exit code, no record anywhere. That is deliberate, because the runner is silent by design and a bookkeeping failure must not break a sync, and it is also exactly why the `$null` marshalling defect survived for the life of the file. It means any future `File.Replace` failure freezes the state file the same way with the same absence of signal, and two triggers remain reachable: a store root on a network path or a non-NTFS volume, and a sharing violation from a concurrent reader. The section that fixed the marshalling deliberately left the catch alone, because the remedy is a design question rather than an edit: the state file is the only channel the runner has, so a failure to write it cannot be reported through it, and each candidate (a sibling error file, a marker the SessionStart hook notices, a non-zero exit whose caller ignores it anyway) buys signal at the cost of the silence the runner exists to keep. Not urgent, since the one known trigger is fixed and the two remaining ones need an unusual store location. Worth settling before anyone adds a third consumer to that file.

- **The store's auto-sync stands down on a cross-machine MEMORY.md conflict: the root-cause diagnosis held, every conclusion drawn from the state file did not, and all three open items are now closed (2026-08-19, corrected 2026-08-21 from the sync-state-writer-and-push-pair effort).** The reading that opened this item was `~/.claude/kit-sync-state.json` on this machine showing `lastOk` empty, `lastResult: transient`, `reason: push-failed`, with the store ahead 4 / behind 31 of `origin/main`, and the diagnosis behind it stands: a genuine cross-machine content conflict, both machines having appended index lines to `memory-operator/MEMORY.md` and both having created `memory-operator/claude-code-bash-rule-token-matching.md` (an add/add), so a rebase of the local commits onto `origin/main` conflicted and the sync correctly stood down. That is the parent spec's own Out-of-Scope item #3 (auto-resolving cross-machine index and add/add conflicts is deferred to union-merge work), so it was expected behavior rather than a regression, and nothing corrupt reached origin. Everything the entry then concluded needs correcting. Its manual-reconcile item is done, by exactly the remedy it named: the store's reflog carries `refs/remotes/origin/main@{2026-08-20 20:45:43}: pull --rebase: fast-forward` followed by `update by push` at 20:46:50, and the store now reads `main` tracking `origin/main` at 0 ahead / 0 behind. Its open question, why the recorded reason was `push-failed` rather than `pull-conflict`, is answered, and the hypothesis it offered is refuted by the code: the guess was that the runner resolves `@{upstream}` to a fixed commit before the fetch, making the rebase a no-op against a stale base, but `sync-store.ps1` fetches at line 459 and resolves `@{upstream}` at line 469, in that order and in the same block, so the resolve always reads a freshly fetched ref. The real answer is the state writer's null-marshalling defect: `Write-SyncState` can write the file only once, through the `File.Move` branch that runs when no state file exists, so every later write is silently dropped and the recorded reason freezes at whichever reason happened to be current at that first write, `pull-conflict` included. And its central claim, that "the shipped `doctor/sync-store.ps1` has never once succeeded", is not merely unsupported but false. That claim was drawn from `lastOk` being empty, which a file whose only landed write is its first cannot ever set afterward. The reflog shows the runner's own signature sequence, a `fetch --quiet: fast-forward` followed by a rebase and an `update by push`, at 2026-08-21 00:29:58 through 00:30:01 and again at 09:26:12 through 09:26:16, with two further commit-and-push cycles at 10:05 and 10:08; `fetch` and `push` each appear exactly once in the whole `plugins/claude-kit/doctor/` tree, both in `sync-store.ps1` (lines 459 and 548), so nothing else the kit runs produces that trio. Those four successes on 2026-08-21 also confirm the null-marshalling defect on the machine this reading was taken on rather than only on the other syncing machine, because they sit against a `kit-sync-state.json` whose mtime is still 2026-08-19 18:00:05 and whose contents still read `"lastResult":"transient","reason":"push-failed","lastOk":""`: a working runner and a frozen file. What remains is a deployment check, not a diagnosis. The frozen file's `firstFailSince` reads `2026-08-19T22:00:05`, so the seven-day failure-streak nudge in `hooks/memory-session.js` fires on a demonstrably healthy store around 2026-08-26 unless the fixed script reaches both machines first, and since each machine runs the installed plugin copy rather than this clone, confirm on each after the fix ships that `kit-sync-state.json` starts moving and that `firstFailSince` clears.

- **Give the leash bind a compare-and-swap, as the queue advance already has (2026-08-18, from the compaction gate binding effort).** `bindSession` in `kit-goal-lib.js` is an unlocked read-modify-write that rewrites the whole goal state, and its own comment already records that a clear landing between its read and its write can be resurrected by it. The gate now calls it too, and past the compaction trigger the harness re-offers every assistant turn, so an unbound armed goal in a claiming session attempts that write far more often than at stops alone: the window did not open, its cadence widened. The concrete failure is an operator running `/kit-goal clear` at high context while an offer is in flight and finding the leash back on, recoverable by clearing again. `advanceGoal` already carries the fix's shape, refusing when the re-read plan or `armedAt` no longer matches the caller's snapshot, and the timestamp leg is load-bearing there because a re-arm putting the same plan back would otherwise pass a plan-only compare. Not urgent: not attacker-reachable, one command to recover, and the race needs a clear inside a single read-modify-write. Left out of the effort that surfaced it because the fix changes `bindSession`'s contract for its other caller, the Stop hook, which deserves its own round rather than riding a gate change.

- **The doctrine parity gates prove presence only for the bullets that defer (2026-08-18, narrowed 2026-08-23 by the process-rule-repairs effort).** The whole-body comparisons in `test/doctrine-parity.test.js` and `test/output-style-parity.test.js` pass whenever the copies agree, so a line deleted from all three leaves both green. The pinned-phrase fix this item asked for now exists in part: `doctrine-parity.test.js` holds a presence pin per deferring bullet beside the identity check, and the open question of what belongs on the list has an answer the last three efforts converged on rather than argued. Deferral is the criterion. A bullet carrying its own content fails loudly when deleted and needs no pin, while a bullet that moves its operative content to a skill and keeps a pointer fails by going quiet, which is the only green-passing deletion path. The residual gap is therefore scoped rather than total: every content-carrying bullet is still unpinned, deliberately, and the failure mode is a deletion nobody meant, which review normally catches. Reopen only if a content-carrying doctrine rule is ever found missing from both copies.

- **Confirm the intake gap check's declared assumptions actually reached the recap (2026-08-18, operator-only, from the intake-gap-check close-out).** The shipped rule says an assumption a session proceeds on must reach Scott on a surface he reads, and the brainstorming half of that rule is the recap he approves a plan from. The check is one no session can run: it needs the transcript of the session that wrote a spec, and by the time a run executes that spec the approving conversation is gone. Read `archive/claude-kit_intake-gap-check_spec_v1.md`'s own `## Assumptions` section, three entries, and confirm each appeared in the recap approved on 2026-08-18. An entry that did not appear reopens the brainstorming half of the surface rule as a new round; all three appearing closes it. Cheap to fold into the next plan approval instead: read the recap against the spec's `## Assumptions` section at the moment of approval, which is when both are in front of you.

- **Prove route (c), the ask branch, on a gap a model cannot wave off (2026-08-18, from the intake-gap-check proof run).** The proof run established the enumerate-and-declare half of the intake bullet against a genuine pre-change control and left the ask branch unexercised. Both GREEN arms enumerated their gaps, named the operator-owned one first, and declared it with a reversal rather than asking, judging it non-material. The cause the Chapter records is the framing: a scratch module makes almost any gap non-material, so the model routes to (b) honestly and route (c) never fires. A later proof has to remove that escape, which means a brief whose gap is expensive to get wrong (money, a customer-visible policy, a migration that cannot be undone) in a codebase the arm believes is real. Until then the ask branch is shipped on design reasoning alone. Not urgent: the failure mode if the branch is weak is a session declaring where it should have asked, which the close-out surface still shows.

- **Reconcile the close-out assumption filter between the doctrine and finishing-work (2026-08-18, from the intake-gap-check docs curation).** The doctrine bullet says the close-out status carries an assumption "made while I was away"; `finishing-work` step 6 lists every entry on the Chapters' `Assumptions:` lines unconditionally. The behaviours form a safe superset, finishing-work showing more and never less, so nothing is lost today and this is not a defect. What it costs is a future editor reading the doctrine alone and looking for a filter no skill implements, or implementing one. One clause in either text settles it; the open question is which way, since the unconditional list is the one that survives a session that cannot tell whether Scott was watching.

- **The intake gap check does not exist for a live session until the plugin cache updates (2026-08-18, operator-only, from the intake-gap-check close-out).** Same shape as the plugin-update items above and worth folding into the same update. The kit runs from the installed plugin cache, and the doctrine a session reads at every session start is rewritten from that cache, so the new bullet, the brainstorming assumptions block and fresh-eyes step, and the executing-work and finishing-work surfaces are all inert until the update lands. The `blind-reader` and `prose-reviewer` agents the document review battery shipped are in the same position and are not dispatchable at all, which is why this effort's own document sections took the code review pair instead.

- **Give a finished queue's blocker notes a durable home (2026-08-16, from the kit-goal-queue reviews).** When `/kit-goal` arms several plans, each plan that ends on a `BLOCKED:` has its blocker's first line recorded into the goal state's `history`, and the last plan's release deletes that state file outright. So once a queue finishes, the content of every mid-queue blocker survives only in the session's own closing summary, which is a soft guarantee: a session that dies or is compacted before writing that summary leaves the operator with plan paths and no blockers. The `goal-blocked` event fires per plan and carries the plan path but no note, by contract. Two candidate fixes, neither taken because both are event-shape or new-surface changes the queue plan deliberately scoped out: add a note field to the event, or write the history to a `.kit/` sidecar before clearing. Judge it on the first real multi-plan queue that actually blocks somewhere: if the closing summary reliably carries the blockers, this stays closed. Raised by the blind reviewer over Sections 1 to 3, adjudicated as a known limitation rather than a defect and documented in `architecture.md` under Goal release events. **Amended 2026-08-27 by the blocked-escalation effort:** the survives-only-in-the-closing-summary premise is false for the window a goal is armed. The machine-coordinator seat's BLOCKED funnel reads a blocked project's goal state through the goal CLI and recovers the recorded note from its history, so a mid-queue blocker has a second reader for as long as the queue is live, and `architecture.md` was corrected in the same effort. What this item still owns is the narrower half, unchanged: the last plan of a queue records no note at all, and the state file is deleted when the queue completes and the goal clears, so the durable home is still missing once that happens.

- **Turn boundary-gated compaction on, then judge it on a real run (2026-08-15, operator-only, from the boundary-gated-compaction close-out).** **Order matters, and getting it backwards is worse than doing nothing: update the installed plugin first, then set the window.** The gate runs from the installed plugin cache, which at close-out was still at an older commit and carried no compaction hooks at all (verified). Setting `autoCompactWindow` against a cache with no gate makes the harness compact early *and* mid-chapter, which is strictly worse than the late-but-unmanaged status quo. So: update the plugin, confirm the cache carries `hooks/kit-compact-gate.js`, and only then set the window. The gate does nothing until `autoCompactWindow` is configured, and writing that value is a change to live machine config, so it waits on `.\doctor.ps1 -Fix` (it offers 285,000, backs the file up, verifies, and aborts rather than clobbering). That value, the 800,000 safety ceiling, and the 10-minute checkpoint bound were re-tuned on 2026-08-15 after the originals were found sized for a 200,000-token window when plan sessions run on roughly 1,000,000; see `archive/claude-kit_compaction-window-retune_spec_v1.md`. A machine carrying any other configured value reaches 285,000 through a `-Fix` re-run, which the doctor's mismatch report names; that one prompt is withheld from `-Yes`, because replacing a value the operator chose is not something an unattended run can judge. Once it is set, the verification is a real multi-day unattended plan run, which no session can produce: the scrollback should show compactions landing right after Chapter commits rather than mid-section, and the stuck or circling stretches that motivated the effort should shorten. Two outcomes reopen work rather than closing this: a run that wedges near the context limit with compaction still deferred means the safety valve failed its one job and reopens the gate, and compactions still landing mid-chapter means the checkpoint or its expiry is mistuned. A third thing to watch rather than act on: the ceiling assumes the roughly 1,000,000-token window plan sessions run on, and on a materially smaller one the valve would never fire and the gate could hold a session against its hard limit, which nothing detects. First real-run data (2026-08-17, a 12-hour two-plan run on the machine that is not the one this repository is checked out on): the gate held mid-section throughout and the valve landed the compaction, so both mechanisms did their job; but no checkpoint was ever opened, because the session was executing a compaction-truncated copy of executing-work that kept step 0 (clear) and lost step 8 (open), so the compaction fell wherever the valve fired, mid-section. That is a session-side omission the re-load instruction added the same day addresses (doctrine, executing-work, and the compact-source SessionStart block), not a gate defect; the next real run is still the verification, now with the re-load in place. The scrollback this item asks you to read is now a file instead: `.kit/compact-gate.jsonl` in the run's own project records one line per auto-compaction verdict, so "did compactions land at boundaries or at the valve" is answered by reading the `reason` field of each `allow` (`checkpoint` is a boundary landing, `valve` is not) rather than by scrolling. Second real-run data point (2026-08-25, this repo, the compaction-deferral plan's own leashed run): two `deny-boundary` records, then an `allow` with `reason: checkpoint` honoring a 3260-second-old checkpoint on the pending leg, so the compaction landed at the declared boundary and the checkpoint was opened this time. Both failure directions this item watches for stayed clear. It remains open because its verification is a multi-day unattended run and this is a single-session observation.

- **Make the compact-source SessionStart block carry a computed state slice, not only an instruction to re-read (2026-08-18, same read).** On `compact`, `session-start.js` tells the session to re-load the governing skill and re-read the plan doc, and lists each In Progress plan's path and commit model (`session-start.js:382-391`); everything past that is left to the model's next tool calls, which is the step the summary-truncated run in the kaizen note skipped for four sections. The candidate: fold a small deterministic slice from the durable artifacts into the block itself, computed by code rather than recalled by the model: the armed plan's current section (first heading whose Status is not Complete), the latest Chapter's `Next:` line, whether a compaction checkpoint is open, and the goal queue position. Bounded to a few lines so it never becomes a second copy of the plan doc in context; the doc stays the source and the slice is the pointer into it. Judge it on the same signal as the mechanize item: a run after the re-load fix that still resumes on the wrong section or skips the checkpoint. If the prose re-load holds, park it.

- **Confirm the new effort pins are live in the installed plugin cache (2026-08-11, operator-only, from the reviewer-effort-compensation close-out).** The four agent definitions carrying `effort` changes are verified by file content only. The kit runs from an installed plugin cache rather than this working tree, and no session has confirmed the cache picked the new values up; every reviewer dispatched during that effort carried an explicit Workflow effort override, which would mask a stale cache completely. Confirming it needs a plugin update followed by one ordinary Agent-tool reviewer dispatch observed at the expected effort, which is Scott's to run. If the cache does not carry them, the fix is a plugin update rather than a code change, and it reopens nothing: the shipped rule is correct either way, it would just be running at the old defaults until the cache refreshes. **Amended 2026-09-06 by the reviewer-fable-uncap close-out:** five reviewer agents now carry pins (`low` on adversarial-reviewer, blind-reviewer, blind-reader and prose-reviewer, `medium` on security-reviewer), and an Agent-tool reviewer dispatch at frontmatter effort is every per-section round over an opus- or fable-tier writer rather than a special observation, so the confirmation falls out of the worker's first review round after the next `claude plugin update` and restart. **Amended 2026-09-20 by the reviewer re-ranking plan:** the performance reviewer joins them at `medium`, so six reviewer agents carry pins.

- **Comparative lessons-learned across the three repos' update waves (parked 2026-08-25).** When the claude-kit, discord-channels, and ai-os update efforts now in flight have all finished, run one comparative retrospective over their records (Chapters, kaizen notes, the peer-messaging transcripts) rather than three separate close-outs. The operator's framing to carry in verbatim: the strongest observed result of the 2026-08-25 peer-messaging experiment was recursive improvement, context spread across warm sessions with different expertise feeding retrospection, capture, and planning through the kit machinery, against the prior cycle of backlog-then-eventually-cold-session with no knowledge of cause. Two questions the retro should answer beyond the comparison: whether consult answers tracked the target's role (an Opus executor gives detailed ground truth, not goals-and-directions altitude, so the mesh may need an explicit altitude map of who to ask what), and which lessons replicated across all three repos versus appearing in only one. Signal: all three efforts reach Complete.
- **The Dispatch Authorization by-reference convention is live; one line at the format's owner remains, post-queue (parked 2026-08-26, signal fired and narrowed 2026-08-27).** The remote is public, and the format definition (`plugins/claude-kit/skills/kit-goal/SKILL.md:46`) asks only who authorized the run, when, and which sessions, saying nothing about quotation. The sweep the original entry envisioned has no editable live surface left: the three plans carrying a grant today record it by reference (date, scope, the artifact holding the operator's words; landed at `21d68c9` and armed on that form the same day, which is the fired signal), the two live operator quotations a pattern-plus-control grep of `docs/plans/` found outside authorization sections (`claude-kit_session-roles_notes_v1.md`) were converted in the same change, and every quoted grant now sits in `docs/archive/` (coordinator-and-roles, compact-boundaries, dispatch-authority), immutable history whose innocuous approval sentences are in the public git history irrecoverably either way. Residue: one sentence at the format's owner stating the convention (in a public repository the grant is recorded by reference, date, scope, and artifact, never quotation), deferred because a plugin-payload edit while the five-plan queue runs stales the build stamp the leashed worker gates on. Signal: the armed queue completes.
- **Turn the in-context advisor off, and update the plugin (2026-08-15, operator-only, from the consult close-out).** Two machine actions the kit cannot take for itself. First, `/advisor off`, or remove the `advisorModel` line from `~/.claude/settings.json`; QA confirmed at close-out that it is still set to `opus` on this machine, and the kit never edits live machine settings. Second, update the installed plugin: every shipped change in that effort lives in the plugin cache, which is still at a pre-effort commit, so the consult skill and the consultant agent do not exist for a live session until the update lands, and the doctrine a session reads is rewritten from that cache at every session start. Order does not matter between the two, but the advisor flip is the one with a reopening condition: if long-run quality visibly degrades with no consults firing, that outcome reopens the trigger wording rather than the decommission.

- **Consult adoption watch (2026-08-15, from the consult close-out).** The consult's trigger floor was set by judgment, not by data: a counted floor (a second failed attempt, any decision-shaped BLOCKED, a debugging dead end) plus a general license, deliberately chosen to be recognizable by a re-reader rather than to depend on a stuck session's own sense of being stuck. Judge it on the first real long run, on three signals the run produces for free: the `consults <n>` counts in the Chapter Metrics lines, whether every BLOCKED that reached the operator arrived with a ruling attached, and how many rounds burned before the first consult fired. The two failure shapes point opposite ways and the fix differs. A BLOCKED with no ruling and no consult in the Metrics, or a third same-tier attempt on one problem, means the floor is too narrow or the wording is not firing, and reopens the trigger wording in `consult/SKILL.md` and `executing-work/SKILL.md`. Consults firing on questions the spec already answered means it is too wide, and the floor tightens. Do not widen or tighten speculatively; this item owns that call and it waits on a real run.

- **Open experiment: Haiku implementer-tier band (from kaizen, 2026-07-03; kept on the operator's call, decided 2026-08-22).** The haiku transcription band shipped ahead of demand, so its edges are provisional. First recorded data point (2026-08-22 sweep of every `Implemented By:` line in docs/, since transcript stores rotate out in weeks and Chapters are the durable dispatch record): one dispatch ever, `archive/claude-kit_hook-canary-and-goal-events_spec_v1.md` Chapter 3 (2026-07-25, commit 174991b), a faithful clone on the first attempt with 0 NEEDS_CONTEXT and 0 escalations, though its per-section review pair was skipped under the trivial-section rule, so the driving signal below has still never literally fired; the quality evidence there is the controller's full-diff read and a doctor PASS. The 2026-07-07 to 07-14 workstation corpus had zero (nothing was tiered haiku). Driving signal unchanged: haiku-tier sections passing first-round adversarial review keep the band; escalations to sonnet are the mis-band counter; do not widen speculatively. The same-day qa-verifier opus-to-sonnet re-pin reverts with one line in `agents/qa-verifier.md` if finishing passes start missing acceptance-criterion nuance.
- **Tune the memory store's invented constants on real evidence (2026-07-31).** `EXTEND_PER_APPLIED_DAY = 30` and `EXTEND_CAP_DAYS = 365` (`plugins/claude-kit/scripts/memq.js:497-498`), both digest budgets, `RECALL_MAX_LINES = 200` and `RECENT_MAX_LINES = 200` (`:379-380`), the successor display cap `SUPERSEDED_SHOWN = 4` (`:378`), `recent`'s `RECENT_DEFAULT_SINCE = '1d'` window (`:8350`), and the neighbour floor `NEIGHBOUR_FLOOR = 0.30` (`:564`), the similarity at which the authoring verbs' neighbours block and the decay scan's pairs block read two records as one fact, tuned at a decay pass that has a pairs block to read, were seeded by judgment with zero observations behind them, the same posture as the haiku implementer band above: tune on real numbers, do not widen speculatively. The instrument that produces those numbers now exists but has not accrued: nearly every record in this project's store scans `applied never`. The applied stamp is a self-report a session makes with `memq touch --applied`, and the store holds almost none, so the reinforcement formula is shipped, correct, and currently inert. Two separate triggers, neither of them a date. For the two extension constants, the first `decay-scan` whose standing usage line reports applied stamps across several files: check whether real tallies push memories past the thresholds too fast or not fast enough, and whether the 365-day cap ever binds on anything. For either digest budget, the first digest that prints a counted remainder at all, since a truncation only ever appears when the budget binds and it names which surface it cut. For the default window, a close-out whose session ran longer than a day and whose digest therefore had to be re-run with an explicit `--since`. The archive index's unbounded growth is the related pressure on the same surface: `recall` caps its read at 64 KB and says so when the cap binds, which converts that growth into a bounded read rather than a bounded digest, so a clip note is the signal to trim the index by hand. The same posture covers `memq.js`'s six semantic-ranking constants (`SEMANTIC_SHOWN`, `SEMANTIC_FLOOR`, `SEMANTIC_APPLIED_BOOST`, `SEMANTIC_BOOST_CAP_DAYS`, `SEMANTIC_ARCHIVE_DEMOTION`, `SEMANTIC_SUPERSEDED_DEMOTION`, at `plugins/claude-kit/scripts/memq.js:324-329`): seeded by judgment against one measurement of one model before any real semantic-search usage existed, so tune them against the first `find` runs whose rankings can be checked against felt relevance, not on a schedule.

- **A memory used mostly from other projects under-accrues applied stamps here (2026-08-03, from the synced-semantic-memory finishing reviews).** `memq touch --applied` resolves only the caller's own tiers, so a shared-tier memory another project applies constantly but this one merely reads takes no stamp from this project's sessions. The clock still ages it correctly for every project that does apply it, so this is a demotion risk for one under-using project's view rather than a loss: another project's stamps keep it alive machine-wide. Not a defect, since `touch` resolving only the caller's tiers is the scoping every memq command uses; flagged so a memory that scans idle from one vantage is checked with `memq find` across stores before being archived by hand.
- **Extend the integrity manifest beyond hooks/ (2026-07-31, from the section 2 security review).** The build's hash manifest covers the shipped hook files only, but the same user-writable plugin cache ships other executed payload with the same rationalized-edit exposure and no detection: `scripts/memq.js` and `scripts/memq-shim.js` (whose output is injected into session context and whose type-tier fencing the security model treats as a control) and `doctor/*.ps1`. One member still changes the sizing rather than lengthening the list, and it is no longer the watcher: `doctor/sync-store.ps1` is what the kit executes unwatched. `memory-session.js` spawns it detached, with its streams discarded and the child unreferenced, and it fetches, rebases, commits and pushes the memory store to a private remote out of that same writable cache. Its trigger is an attended session start, but nothing watches the run and no canary sees an in-place edit to it, because the manifest does not reach `doctor/` either. That is a narrower case than the watcher was, since the trigger is at least attended, and it is not the attended-session one this item was scoped for. Deliberate scope choice in the backlog-sweep effort, not a defect; extend the manifest and canary walk to those directories in a later pass. Ride-along from the same review: `build.sh`'s stamp block has never run end to end on a POSIX box (verified here by transcription and a read-only fragment run; the shasum and openssl fallback branches have run nowhere), so give it a one-time smoke on WSL or Linux before anything relies on a build.sh-produced stamp - the natural moment is the doctor.sh Linux-sibling item below.
- **Tag the multi-sidecar readers' malformed-line notes (2026-08-04, from the find-legibility finishing reviews).** `readUsage` takes an optional tier tag that rides in its stderr notes, so a run reading several sidecars can say which one held the bad line. `memq unstamped` passes it, and `decay-scan` was tagged on 2026-08-25 by this repository's kaizen batch, whose section 10 gave that scan a candidate-suppression clause that sends an operator to remove the exact line, so an ambiguous note there had become actively misleading. Three commands still emit the bare `skipping malformed usage line N`, which names no file to go fix: `recall` (three or four sidecars), `recent` (one per tier), and `find`, which is the widest of them all and the one to do first: its `tallyForTier` reads a sidecar per tier per store across every project store on the machine, so its note is ambiguous across stores as well as tiers. One argument per call site, plus a store segment in `find`'s tag.
- **`memq recent`'s added-versus-updated split does not mean what it reads as (2026-08-01, found by running the shipped command against the real store).** The label is correct against its stated rule and its tests, and still misleads: the Edit and Write tools rewrite a memory file rather than patching it in place, which resets `birthtimeMs` to the moment of the edit, so a memory that has existed for a week and was edited today reports `added`. Measured on this store: `subagent-wait-mechanics.md` and `plugin-cache-snapshot-inert-hooks.md`, both days old and both edited in that close-out, carry a birthtime equal to their mtime to the millisecond. The platform gate is doing its job (Windows keeps a real creation time); what the birthtime tracks is the writing tool's behavior, not the record's history, so on this toolchain `added` effectively means "written by a tool that recreates the file" and fires for nearly every memory write. No test caught it because fixtures construct files directly. Three ways out, cheapest first: drop the split and label every memory file `updated`, which is true either way and costs the platform gate; key `added` on the memory's own `created:` frontmatter date falling inside the window (`readFrontmatterCreated` already exists and `lastAliveMs` already reads it), which is author-asserted and absent on some records; or keep the split and say plainly in `memory-system` that `added` means first written by this toolchain. The middle option is the recommended one, since the frontmatter date is the only signal that describes the record rather than the tool.
- **Fence `memq recall`'s journal lines under a store pin (2026-08-01, from the memq-session-recap finishing reviews).** `cmdRecall` prints journal lines at column zero whatever the pin state, so under a pin each carries the latest summary another of the instance's workers wrote for that key, in the tool's own voice. `memq recent` fences its journal lines for exactly this reason, and the security model's keyed-on-the-pin rule reaches both. Deliberately deferred rather than missed: `recall`'s surface is narrower (one line per key against `recent`'s one per entry inside a window), and closing it changes a shipped command's output and its expected-output tests, which is a separate call from adding a new command. The counterweight is frequency, and it argues for doing it: `recall` runs at every effort start while `recent` runs at close-out. The fix is one `projectIndent` on the journal line at `plugins/claude-kit/scripts/memq.js:3486`, plus the recall tests' expected output and the exception paragraph in `docs/security-model.md`.
- **Workstation (ASR) config repairs (2026-07-15 verification pass).** A user-level graphify PreToolUse hook is dead machine-wide and noisy - it fires on virtually every tool call and exits 127 with the mangled path `C:UsersASR.localbingraphify.EXE` (backslashes stripped before reaching bash), so the guard silently never runs; fix the hook command quoting or remove the hook.
- **Explore channels for unattended interactive runs (research preview) (2026-08-23, parked 2026-07-08).** Kept and re-scoped: a gated channel relay is now live in-session, delivering operator messages from a Discord thread through an allowlist that names one account, so the "is a channel usable at all" half of this exploration is answered by a running system rather than by a prototype. What is unexplored is the other two halves the item named, a scheduler or webhook nudging an overnight run's next turn, and permission relay answering tool-approval prompts from a phone. Neither has been stood up. A channel is an MCP server that injects external events into the interactive session as model input (code.claude.com/docs/en/channels-reference). Two uses for long runs, both keeping the work on subscription billing: a local webhook or scheduler nudging an overnight run's next turn, and permission relay (v2.1.81+) answering tool-approval prompts from a phone. A channel cannot execute `/resume` (events are conversation input, not UI commands), so it nudges a live session rather than starting or swapping one. Caveats: custom channels need `--dangerously-load-development-channels` at every session start while off the allowlist, senders must be gated (an ungated channel is a prompt-injection vector), and undelivered events drop silently. Decided 2026-07-08: prototype when the next overnight-run need materializes.
- **A `doctor.sh` sibling for WSL/Linux (2026-07-09).** The Windows doctor is verified on both machines (see the Q3 snapshot); the frictions it fixes were all Windows-shaped, so the Linux sibling was deliberately skipped. When it lands it should absorb `setup.sh` the way doctor's `-Fix` absorbed setup.ps1/setup.cmd on Windows, leaving doctor as the single first-run tool everywhere.
- **Push-time stranded-branch guard (candidate, 2026-07-17).** When a repo auto-deletes merged head branches, a late push to a just-merged branch silently recreates it off the integration line (the stranded-commit trap), signaled only by `git push` printing `[new branch]` for a branch that should already exist. The kit already ships merge-strand-guard and branch-hygiene, and the manual strand-check caught a live instance on the neo project, so this is defense-in-depth, not a gap. A mechanical guard would flag an unexpected `[new branch]` on push. Build only if the trap recurs despite the existing guards; the strand-check working is why it is parked, not prioritized.


- **Open experiment: effort dials and reviewer tiering in flight (2026-07-29; dials set 2026-07-31).** The effort knob exists after all, as subagent frontmatter rather than per-dispatch: `effort` (low/medium/high/xhigh/max, overrides session effort; code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents.md). Current pins, after the 2026-08-11 revision: `effort: medium` on implementer-sonnet, implementer-opus, qa-verifier, docs-curator; `effort: high` on adversarial-reviewer, blind-reviewer, security-reviewer, implementer-fable, consultant, and, since the document review battery shipped on 2026-08-18, blind-reader and prose-reviewer; no field on implementer-haiku (Haiku 4.5 lacks effort support), council-member (it runs in-line during a brainstorm, where the session's own effort is the right call), design-facilitator. The same change added the reviewer model rule (executing-work step 3, finishing-work step 3): per-section reviewers one tier above the writer, finishing adversarial review always fable. A frontmatter dial cannot split per-section from finishing reviews (same agent type). **Partly closed by the reviewer-effort-compensation effort (2026-08-11).** The claim flagged here as most likely wrong, that the finishing gate riding fable-at-medium was acceptable, was retired rather than tested: reviewers now pin `effort: high`, and effort is chosen per dispatch from the model the reviewer actually runs at and whether that model is the aimed-at tier or a stand-in for one above it. The split this item called unreachable is reachable, by a route the note never considered: a per-dispatch effort override does not exist on the Agent tool and never did, but `Workflow`'s `agent()` carries `model`, `effort`, and `agentType` per call, and an `agentType` dispatch there stays governed by the read-only guard. What stays open is the measurement, unchanged: judge the new levels on Chapter Metrics against the pre-change baseline (review rounds, NEEDS_CONTEXT, escalations, findings quality). Revert is one frontmatter line per agent plus the table rows. The Haiku implementer band above is still untested and still waiting on its own data. **Amended 2026-08-19 by the reviewer-tier-cap effort:** the per-section bump is now capped at opus and the security-reviewer is inside the rule rather than outside it, with one exception keeping it off fable per-section. That change folds into this experiment rather than opening a second one, so the levels being judged are the post-cap levels; the pre-change baseline table it is judged against is in Chapter 1 of `docs/archive/claude-kit_reviewer-tier-cap_spec_v1.md`. The effort generator described earlier in this item is superseded by the same change: effort now turns on whether the reviewer's model sits above the writer's tier, not on the aimed-at-versus-stand-in distinction, so an Opus reviewer reaches `max` whenever it has no headroom over what it is reading. **Amended 2026-09-05 by the reviewer-fable-uncap effort:** the cap is retired and every per-section reviewer runs at Fable, effort by lens (`low` for the code and document pairs, `medium` for the security-reviewer, finishing unchanged at `high`), on the operator's reading that Fable 5.1 at `low` barely moves the allotment and that review rounds are where runs spin. The per-section effort now lives in the reviewer agents' frontmatter, the finishing pass takes the Workflow route to `high`, and the Opus `max` row is back to its 2026-08-11 ground, unavailability alone. The levels being judged are these; the post-cap baseline they are judged against is in Chapter 1 of `docs/archive/claude-kit_reviewer-fable-uncap_spec_v1.md`, and its Chapter 2 carries the per-row table. When reading later Chapter Metrics, count the rounds in which a `low` reviewer made no tool call at all as their own class: the one known trait of Fable 5.1 at `low` is a reluctance to call tools, and a diff reviewer that read no file returned a green it did not earn. **Amended 2026-09-06 by the reviewer-tier-hybrid effort:** before the uncap reached any worker, the operator's weekly Fable reading under the capped rule was a few percent ahead of pace, so the per-section reviewer returns to one tier above the writer, with Fable rather than Opus as the ceiling: opus- and fable-tier sections keep the Fable reviewer at the uncap's frontmatter efforts, and a haiku- or sonnet-tier section's reviewer (Sonnet or Opus) runs at `high` through the Workflow route. The levels being judged are these, against the same Chapter 2 baseline; no worker round having run under the uncap on this machine, it produced no Chapter Metrics. The next lever if the bars keep climbing is the Opus cap over opus-tier sections, per `docs/plans/claude-kit_reviewer-tier-hybrid_spec_v1.md`. **Amended 2026-09-10 by the review-tier-decay effort (`docs/archive/claude-kit_review-tier-decay_spec_v1.md`, stage 2 of the lean kit program, carrying its decision 6):** the one-tier-up roster now holds for a section's first review round alone, and every later round runs one lens at the writer's own tier with sonnet as the floor, the security lens joining no decayed round; a Critical that survives adjudication re-raises the round after it to round 1's roster and tier, and a re-raised round returning none hands the next round back to one lens. The levels being judged are these, against the same Chapter 2 baseline, and the measurement this answers is the review rounds per section the program read as rising from about one to over four. **Amended 2026-09-21 by the reviewer re-ranking plan:** the performance reviewer joins the roster as a second advisory lens at `medium`, riding round 1 and a re-raised round on its own trigger list exactly as the security lens does, and neither advisory lens joins a decayed round; the correctness pair's levels are unchanged, so the baseline this experiment is judged against is the same.
- **Pin executing-work's reviewer-effort table rows to the agent frontmatter map (2026-09-05).** `test/readonly-agent-guard.test.js` pins each reviewer's frontmatter effort to a literal map, and the table at executing-work step 3 carries the same values as a third copy nothing reads, so the table can drift from the frontmatter while the suite stays green. Have the test parse the table's backticked efforts and compare them to the map. Deferred from the reviewer-fable-uncap effort because that test file sits at its line cap and the budget file was another session's in-flight edit.
- **State in `docs/security-model.md` that the finishing route's read-only guard coverage is conditional on the Workflow `agentType` field (2026-09-05).** The finishing reviews now take `Workflow`'s `agent()` by default rather than only as the compensation exception, and no hook matches the Workflow tool, so the read-only guard binds a finishing reviewer only where the orchestrator fills `agentType` as the Reviewer Dispatch template requires. The guard's coverage in code is unchanged; what an auditor cannot see from the security model is that the finishing security pass now rests on that instruction-level field by default. One sentence naming the precondition, beside the guard's existing description. Deferred from the reviewer-fable-uncap effort's finishing security review because that file was another session's in-flight edit. Under the reviewer-tier-hybrid rule (2026-09-06) the per-section rounds over haiku- and sonnet-tier writers, their security-reviewer included, take the same route, so the precondition covers those passes too, and under the review-tier-decay rule (2026-09-10) a later round's one lens takes it over an opus writer as well. Brainstorming's step 10 plan review takes it as well, dispatching `plan-reviewer` through `Workflow`'s `agent()` before a spec is written Ready, so the sentence owed here covers a pre-arming dispatch and not only the review rounds.
- **Install the reviewer-fable-uncap and reviewer-tier-hybrid rules on this machine (2026-09-06, operator-only, from the two close-outs).** Run `claude plugin update`, then restart the KIT: Worker session, since a running session reads skills and agents from its frozen plugin view and every review it runs until then is under the retired Opus cap; then watch the Fable usage bars over the next few worker sections. The next lever is the Opus cap over opus-tier sections, one commit over the hybrid plan's section 1 files, and the open reviewer-tiering experiment above is where the levels are judged.

- **Fable 5 prescriptiveness audit, trigger-based (2026-07-29).** Anthropic's Fable 5 guidance says skills written for prior models are often too prescriptive for it and can degrade output, and that brief instructions now steer as well as enumerated case lists. The kit's heavy enumerations (the completion contract's rationalization table and red-flags list, the dispatch-brief template) each trace to an observed failure, so nothing is thinned speculatively; the writing-skills bar cuts both ways. The trigger: a Fable-led run observed fighting or over-applying a specific enumerated rule makes that rule the candidate to thin, RED/GREEN-tested like any behavior-shaping change.

- **Kaizen scoping companion against over-generalization (deferred 2026-07-26).** The kaizen skill's "State the lesson, not the incident" rule generalizes captured notes; the symmetric failure (Twain's cat: over-generalizing to "never touch stoves") has a pre-written fix, a companion line "Generalize to the cause, not the scene. The lesson covers everything hot, and nothing that's merely a stove." Deliberately left out per writing-skills' observed-failure bar. Add it only when an actually over-generalized kaizen note or memory is observed; the "one level more general" phrasing already bounds the swing, which is why this is expected to stay parked.

- **Decide whether the read-only guard should deny outbound-network commands (2026-08-18, from the document-review-battery security review).** The guard's strict class denies write-shaped commands and says nothing about `curl`, `wget`, `Invoke-WebRequest`, `Invoke-RestMethod`, or `nslookup`, and the tree-state capture that backstops a review round sees only worktree deltas, which a network call never leaves. So the sole control against a read-only reviewer being talked into exfiltrating by a document it was told to review is the charter sentence telling it not to. All three document-reading agents (the two document reviewers and the plan reviewer) state that prohibition with its rationale, which is the strongest form the kit's model currently offers, and the exposure predates them; what changed is that three agents now exist whose entire job is reading documents that may come from outside. The candidate is naming the outbound-network commands in the strict-class denylist, which is cheap and mechanical. The argument against is that a read-only reviewer sometimes has a legitimate reason to fetch (a version, a published doc), so the denial would need an escape and the escape is where this gets expensive. Judge it the next time a reviewer agent is given a document from a source the operator does not control.

- **Decide whether the disclosure sweep needs a section-time trigger (2026-08-18, from the document-review-battery adversarial review).** The disclosure sweep runs at finishing, because that is where the audience predicate lives. Under Commit-and-Push, a section closes by committing and pushing, so an outward-facing document carrying a leak is already on the remote before the sweep that would catch it ever runs. The executing-work security-reviewer trigger list (input handling, auth, SQL construction, secrets or configuration, an external boundary) does not name a section whose spec carries a `Disclosure:` list, so nothing pulls the sweep earlier. This matches what the spec designed rather than drifting from it, which is why it was recorded rather than fixed at close-out; the proof runs are the counter-evidence, since they exercised the sweep at what was effectively section scope and it fit naturally there. The fix, if taken, is one entry on that trigger list. Judge it on the first real effort that ships an outward-facing document under Commit-and-Push.


- **Silent store auto-sync: the fail-safe hardening deferred at ship (2026-08-19, from the worktree-store-and-autosync Section 2 close-out, `docs/plans/claude-kit_worktree-store-and-autosync_spec_v1.md`).** The auto-sync shipped after five review rounds, security-clean of any Critical or security-Major (the inbound screen converged to a completeness-proven mode+path allowlist). The operator authorized shipping now with these residuals parked (Option A, 2026-08-19); every one is fail-safe (it wedges the sync loudly or misses a nudge, never leaks or silently corrupts) and most need a party who can push to the private remote. Judge each on real multi-machine use; none is urgent. (1) **Intermediate-commit history wedge:** the inbound screen reads the upstream TIP tree, so an add-then-remove of a disallowed path across incoming commits presents a clean tip, the rebase latches the intermediate commit into local history, and the post-rebase probe then wedges a loud `leaks` gate (push blocked, no leak). The complete fix screens the incoming range, which is fragile across merge commits; the tip screen covers the common case. (2) **Hostile managed-file intake:** the screen admits an incoming `.gitignore`/`.gitattributes` without a content check, so a peer's non-canonical one materializes over the live allowlist, the drift gate blocks the push, and `doctor -Fix` refuses to rewrite a Foreign managed file, so the loud line's own remedy cannot clear it (the operator fixes the file by hand). Fix: screen the two managed files' content (canonical or marker) on intake. (3) **Overridden-root silence on win32:** a pending store at a non-default `KIT_MEMORY_ROOT` gets neither the spawn (by design) nor any text (the state-file and marker paths never run there), so its drift is silent, where off-Windows and the base hook nudge. Fix: emit the text fallback for a pending owned non-default-root store; the cost that parked it is that the transient-streak and backstop tests use non-default stores as proxies and would need reworking onto default-store fixtures. (4) **Outbound probes' own blindness:** `install-memory-sync.ps1`'s tracked/history leak probes read paths only (no mode axis) and dedupe objects, the exact two gaps the inbound screen just closed, so a mode-120000 entry or a rename in local history could publish; same-user reachable, low value, and in the shared installer the section otherwise left alone. (5) **PATH steering of the bare `git`:** the detached sync invokes `git` by name, so a repo-carried PATH could choose the binary the unattended credential-using run executes, the same env-steering class as the `USERPROFILE` anchor the section did close; the fix is an absolute git path across the sync's invocations.

- **Type-check the session-id comparison the leash and the compaction gate both rest on (2026-08-21, from the boundary-ritual-reinforcement Section 2 security review).** `sameSessionId` in `plugins/claude-kit/hooks/kit-compact-lib.js` coerces both sides with `String()` before comparing, so a non-string `session_id` can compare equal: a payload carrying the bound id inside an array fired the chapter-boundary nudge in the reviewer's probe. The nudge's own consequence is one spurious reminder, which is why it was not fixed inside that section, but the same comparator decides the PreCompact gate's verdict and the Stop hook's leash binding, where a type-confused match is worth considerably more. Not reachable from a real harness payload, which always sends a string, so this is hardening against a shared primitive rather than a live defect. The fix is one line, requiring `typeof` string on both sides, and its cost is that every caller must be checked for a non-string it currently gets away with.

- **The integrity manifest reports on the files it knows, and is silent about a hook it does not (2026-08-21, from the boundary-ritual-reinforcement Section 2 security review).** `integrityProbe` in `plugins/claude-kit/hooks/hook-canary.js` iterates the build manifest's own keys, so a `.js` file present in the installed plugin cache but absent from the manifest is uncovered silently rather than reported. The manifest is currently complete, and the build regenerates it, so nothing is wrong today; what is missing is the reverse check that would notice a hook file the manifest does not name. This matters more now that a PostToolUse hook writes into the model's context on every edit, which raises the value of a tamper against the cache from one injection per session to one per edit.

- **The checkpoint CLI cannot tell a dispatched subagent from its parent (2026-09-06, parked 2026-08-21, from the boundary-ritual-reinforcement Section 2 security review; narrowed at the checkpoint-session-validation close-out).** The session-level half of this item shipped: `kit-compact-checkpoint.js open` and `clear` now refuse a caller that is not the session the checkpoint blesses, and the gate honors only a record whose opener matches the compacting session. What remains is the subagent leg: a dispatched agent's shell carries the parent session's own id, so the caller check passes for one, and the chapter-boundary nudge's stand-down on the payload's agent keys is the only guard that can tell them apart, resting on a harness field established by probe. The 2026-09-06 run also saw the live consequence: the gate's log recorded allow-checkpoint decisions under the parent's id while the parent never compacted, which fits a subagent's auto-compaction spending the leash holder's single-shot checkpoint (inferred from `.kit/compact-gate.jsonl`; the PreCompact payload's transcript path would confirm it and the log does not record it). The fix is a refusal in the checkpoint CLI and a subagent leg in the gate keyed on the payload's agent marker, defence in depth beside the nudge.

- **Judge the chapter-boundary nudge on a real leashed run (2026-08-21, operator-only, from the boundary-ritual-reinforcement close-out).** The whole effort is a feedback loop for a failure only a live multi-hour run produces: a leashed session that never loads the executing-work skill, closes Chapters from the doctrine alone, and so never opens a compaction checkpoint. Everything shipped is verified in test and by direct hook invocation, and none of that can answer the question the effort exists for, which is whether the reminder actually changes what a session does. Each machine runs the installed plugin copy rather than this clone, so the check waits on the fix reaching both machines. What to look for on the next real unattended plan run: compactions landing right after Chapter commits rather than mid-section, and, in the scrollback, the gate's boundary note NOT still firing after a Chapter has closed. Two outcomes reopen work rather than closing this. A run where the nudge fires and the session still skips the checkpoint means the reminder is reaching the model and losing to something else, which is a wording or a placement question rather than a mechanism one. A run where the nudge never fires at a real Chapter append means one of the seven guards is standing the hook down wrongly on live payloads, and guard 3's agent-key stand-down is the first suspect, since it rests on payload fields established by a probe against one harness version. This item shares its signal with the older "Turn boundary-gated compaction on, then judge it on a real run" item (2026-08-15), and both are now read from the same file rather than from scrollback: `.kit/compact-gate.jsonl` records one line per auto-compaction verdict, so "did the boundary note keep firing after a Chapter closed" is answered by reading the `reason` on each record instead of scrolling for the note.

- **The docs-write guard's redirect heuristic has named blind spots its test table reads as exhaustive (2026-08-23, from the dormant-feature-removal Section 3 security review; narrowed 2026-08-27 by the testing-discipline effort).** `docs-write-guard.js`'s redirect heuristic misses `>|` (the bash noclobber override, because `|` is excluded from the character class before `docs` and so cannot be consumed) and `tee --append docs/x` (the alternation accepts only the `-a` spelling). It sits inside the heuristic-with-a-Stop-scan-backstop risk `docs/security-model.md` already accepts, and the guard is not a security boundary, so this is not a new exposure. What makes it worth a note is that the case table now living in `test/docs-write-guard.test.js` reads as exhaustive and is not. The two misses were traced through the character classes by hand rather than executed, because the reviewer's own probe command was blocked by `docs-write-guard` for containing a `> docs/` string, so confirm them by running the regex probe from a session the guard does not govern. The push-guard half of this entry is closed: `merged-pr-push-guard.js` now finds the push at a real command position, so the newline, bare `&` and subshell shapes it used to miss are guarded and asserted end to end, and the residue it still carries (a quoted string executed later, a backslash-newline continuation, an interposed runner, and every push after the first in one compound) is asserted by name in `test/merged-pr-push-guard.test.js`. The two remaining push-guard items are dated 2026-08-27 further down this file.

- **The README's payload map reads as complete and is not (2026-08-23, from the dormant-feature-removal Section 4 blind review).** The `plugins/claude-kit/` tree in `README.md` enumerates directory contents file by file, which reads as exhaustive, and two entries are absent: `scripts/memory-index.js`, which the doctor invokes and which has its own test file, and `doctor/sync-store.ps1`, which `memory-session.js` spawns detached and which pushes the memory store to a private remote. The second is the one that costs something, because that script is the kit's remaining unwatched executor and the integrity-manifest item above now turns on it. The `doctor/` block listing six of the seven files in that directory is the shape that misleads, since the one it omits is `sync-store.ps1` itself. The skills half of this item is retired on receipts: `standing-watch/` is listed in the map today, and the skills block now carries an entry per directory on disk. Either add the two entries or say in one line that the map is illustrative; adding them is cheaper and keeps the map's own contract.

- **The frontmatter read has no size bound (parked 2026-08-23, from the memq harness-shape section security review).** `frontmatterField` reads the whole file with `fs.readFileSync(file, 'utf8')`, and `frontmatterBlock` then splits the entire file into lines, while only the first 40 are ever consulted: `FRONTMATTER_MAX_LINES` bounds the scan, not the read. Six readers reach the store through that function, so the bound is missing at each of them. The claim this item opened with, that every frontmatter read in `memq.js` is whole-file, no longer holds: the anchors work gave `listMemories` and `readFrontmatterAnchors` a `readHead(file, FRONTMATTER_READ_CAP)` prefix read, which is this item's own remedy pattern applied at two doors. So the scope is now the readers still going through `frontmatterField`, not the file. Records arrive by git sync from other machines, so an oversized record is inside the threat model; the effect is local memory and CPU amplification only, and past roughly 2 GB `readFileSync` throws and fails safe to `FRONTMATTER_UNREADABLE`. The file already ships the remedy pattern elsewhere: an `fstat` on the descriptor plus a fixed-size prefix read. Pre-existing rather than introduced by that section, which is why it is parked here.

- **Check whether the harness rewrites the pending tier the way it rewrites project memory (parked 2026-08-23, from the memq harness-shape section adversarial review).** The SessionStart hook instructs a session to write provenance frontmatter (`run:`, `vector:`, `section:`, `written:`) at column 0 with the Write tool on records it creates under `memory/pending/<run-id>/`. On Claude Code a Write into a project memory directory is relocated into a `metadata:` map; whether that rewriter reaches the `pending/` subtree is unconfirmed. Nothing in this repo reads those four keys back, since only `tags`, `created`, `pinned`, `machine`, `board`, `supersedes`, `anchors`, `triggers` and `author` go through `frontmatterValue`, so the consequence would land on the external adjudicator that groups a run's writes by `run:`. Confirming it costs one probe: write a record into a pending directory with the Write tool and read it back. If the rewrite does reach it, either the adjudicator learns the two placements the way memq did, or those keys move to a reader that does.

- **The Stop hook's boundary directive sends the model to a session-unscoped deferral report (narrowed 2026-09-23 by the architect; parked 2026-08-25, from the compaction-deferral-signal plan, Section 4 review).** The bare-filename half of this entry, at the Stop hook, the queue-advance reason, the chapter boundary nudge and the finishing-work skill's paragraph, was delivered by `archive/claude-kit_leash-status-truth_spec_v1.md` for the three model-facing sites, which leaves the skill paragraph as prose a session resolves by the plugin-root ladder. What stays here is the second half: `kit-compact-checkpoint.js status`'s deferral-episode report is deliberately session-unscoped, so after a crash and re-arm a dead binding's episode reads as a live hold for up to the four-hour idle bound, which is the state most likely to push an operator into opening a checkpoint mid-chapter. That is a reporting decision, a session-scoped line beside the unscoped one, rather than a defect, and it waits on a ruling.

- **The deferral nudge parses the whole PostToolUse payload before any guard can stand it down (parked 2026-08-24, from the compaction-deferral-signal plan, Section 6 round-5 review).** The hook drains and JSON-parses stdin on every covered tool return, including every dispatched agent's, before the guards that would stand it down can run, and its own header prices the absence of a payload cap without pricing the parse on that hot path. The obvious reorder, checking the external-engine marker before draining stdin, carries its own risk, so this is a measure-then-decide item rather than a known defect: the first move is to measure the parse cost against a real payload rather than to reorder on suspicion.

- **The status-line widget can attribute a Next: line from an interim board to the Chapter above it (parked 2026-08-25, from the compaction-deferral-signal plan's finishing adversarial review).** `parsePlan` in `plugins/claude-kit/scripts/kit-goal-statusline.js` walks the `## Chapters` block and treats a `### Chapter` heading as the boundary between entries, but not a `### Interim board` heading. A column-0 `Next:` line sitting inside a board would therefore be read as the preceding Chapter's, and only where that Chapter carries no `Next:` line of its own. No plan doc in this repo produces that shape, because interim boards write their pointer lines bolded rather than at column zero, so this is a latent parse quirk with no current misrender rather than a live defect. It is recorded because the widget's whole job is to state a run's position accurately on a surface the operator reads at a glance, and a wrong `Next:` there is indistinguishable from a right one. The fix is one more heading in the boundary test.

- **The compaction gate's decision writer has no compare-and-set, so a bystander session can restore a cleared episode (parked 2026-08-25, from the compaction-deferral-signal plan's finishing security review).** `recordGateDecision` in `plugins/claude-kit/hooks/kit-compact-lib.js` does a read-modify-write with no fingerprint verify, unlike the deferral nudge's own stamp, which was given a compare-and-set for exactly this shape. A bystander session's `deny-interactive` carries the deferral episode through from its own read; where that read predates the bound session's safety-valve or illegible allow, writing it back restores the episode the allow just cleared, and a pending checkpoint the allow left standing gets vouched for a second time. Reaching it needs two gate processes in the same project within the same few milliseconds, and the cost is the same bounded one already accepted throughout this mechanism: one mistimed compaction, which is the pre-gate status quo. The residual is now stated in the code beside the undercount note; this item carries the optional hardening, which is to give the writer the `gateOwnedFingerprint` verify the stamp uses. Parked rather than folded because a lock-free writer gaining a retry loop is a change worth its own red-first cases.

- **Confirm the shared-checkout advisory fires with two genuinely live sessions in one working tree (2026-08-26, operator-only, from the dispatch-authority close-out, `docs/archive/claude-kit_dispatch-authority_spec_v1.md`).** Section 4 of that plan added a SessionStart line that says another session may be live in the same checkout, triggered when a sibling transcript in `~/.claude/projects/<flattened-cwd>/` was written inside the last ten minutes. Fifteen fixture tests cover the detector's logic, including the crowded-store case and every fail-open path, and none of them can answer the question the feature exists for, which is whether the line appears in a real second session. It could not be observed during the effort: the only concurrent session on this machine works a different checkout, which flattens to a different directory, so the state that fires the advisory never existed. Each machine runs the installed plugin copy rather than this clone, so the check waits on `claude plugin update`. What to do: with one session already running in a checkout, start a second in that same directory and read its session-start output. What to look for: one line saying another session may be live, carrying a count and an age and no path, session id or filename. Silence in that state reopens Section 4 as a new round, and the first suspect is `ownTranscriptDir`, which trusts a payload `transcript_path` only when its stem matches this session's own id and otherwise falls back to the home store.

- **Confirm the peer-sessions skill and its doctrine bullet actually reach a machine (2026-08-25, operator-only, from the peer-sessions close-out).** After `claude plugin update` on a machine, a fresh session should list `peer-sessions` among its available skills and its loaded doctrine should carry the bullet beginning "Peer sessions are a coordination surface, not a record." Either one absent reopens the install path, not the content: the repo copies are verified present on origin/main (`plugins/claude-kit/skills/peer-sessions/SKILL.md` tracked, the bullet present once in each of the two hand-edited doctrine copies, parity gate green), so an absence is a delivery failure rather than a defect in the change.

- **The shared-tier resolvers collapse a stat failure into absence, so a refused tier leaves the sweep silently (parked 2026-08-25, from the kaizen batch's section 5 round-3 review and its consult).** Three readings decide which tiers exist, and all three collapse a failure into absence. `typedTierOrNull` and `operatorTierOrNull` in `plugins/claude-kit/scripts/memq.js` (around :1024-1031 and :1052-1057) answer `statSync` with `catch { return null }`, so an EACCES or EPERM on a shared-tier directory that genuinely exists is indistinguishable from the directory not existing. `projectType` (:1012-1016) is the third and the most reachable: it swallows any failure reading the project's own `MEMORY.md` as "this project declares no type", so on Windows a transient sharing violation on that file silently drops the whole type tier from every cross-tier command, with no stderr note, because the note that would say which type was declared depends on re-reading the same file. The tier then drops out of `memq unstamped` entirely: no coverage line, no loss flag, and the report can close with its clean-sweep clause while that tier's sidecar holds unadjudicated reads. It also makes the stderr line that says the tier directory does not exist a false statement in the refused case. The store's own doctrine everywhere else is that only ENOENT reads as absence (`readUsage` and `recentFileNames` both carry an explicit three-value status for exactly this reason), so the resolvers are the outlier rather than the pattern. Not fixed inside the kaizen batch, and the reason is scope rather than severity: this is tier *discovery* failing before the sweep runs, not a granularity of the sweep's own readings, and the resolvers are consumed by `recall`, `recent`, `find`, `decay-scan` and `touch --type`, whose single-answer property the code pins deliberately.
